member of the commission. – mr president, colleagues, thank you very much indeed for your comments, questions and suggestions. if i may, let me start with ms hautala’s question on administrative procedures because this is a very fundamental one. the commission is committed to guaranteeing that citizens, businesses and stakeholders can interact with an administration that is open, independent and efficient. for that purpose, the commission, as well as other institutions and bodies, has a well-established set of horizontal rules which govern its administrative behaviour. this includes, among others, a strong commitment made by laying down general provisions in the code of good administrative behaviour, which must guide administrative conduct in relations with citizens, as well as a range of provisions in legislation on staff and financial regulations, on data protection and access to documents, to name but a few. in addition, the annex ‘administrative procedures for the handling of relations with the complainant regarding the application of european union law’ to the communication ‘eu law: better results through better application’, on relations with a complainant in respect of infringements of union law, specifically protects the complainant’s position in the informal phase of the infringement procedure. these measures include: first, a 12-month time limit from the filing of the complaint, within which the commission should, as a general rule, decide whether to initiate an infringement procedure or not; second, the possibility for the complainant to meet the representatives of the commission in order to present his/her argument; and, third, the requirement to give prior notice should the commission intend to close the case, thus allowing the complainant to raise new arguments within four weeks. the commission is, therefore, at this stage not convinced that the benefits of using a legislative instrument that would codify administrative law would outweigh the costs. new legislation would require the revision of a considerable volume of existing european union legislation. even when done with care and a sense of proportion, codification is likely to lead to problems of delimitation between the general and specific laws, not making legislation any clearer or litigation any easier for citizens and businesses affected. it would also remove the flexibility required to adapt to particular needs. the second question concerning the role of parliament in monitoring the implementation of eu law was raised by ms grapini. without prejudice to the democratic control by parliament, under article 17 of the teu it is the commission which oversees the application of european union law, under the control of the court of justice of the european union. the infringement procedure, under articles 258 and 260 teu, form part of a specific sui generis competence conferred directly on the commission under the treaties. therefore, the infringement procedure has a specific bilateral nature. it is conducted solely between the commission and the member state concerned. some members of parliament raised questions about greece and cyprus, especially mr marias, mr mavrides, and others. i would just say that the greek and cypriot memorandums of understanding were not part of european union law because they led to intergovernmental financial support. the greek and cypriot memorandums of understanding were only formally agreed between the lenders and the beneficiary member state. the court of justice confirmed that, when adopting and implementing measures under such an intergovernmental adjustment programme, the national authorities of the concerned member state do not implement european union law. referring to some political statements about double standards and so on, as you know very well, the rule of law, the european union treaties, procedures and competences, are enshrined in a crystal-clear legal framework. here it doesn’t matter whether it is a small country or a big country, we have the same possibility to start these procedures if the situation allows us to do it. the procedure to address infringements is the same for all countries, and the commission takes all cases very seriously, whether it concerns cases in hungary, germany, or any other country. mr president, honourable members, the commission very much shares your interest in the correct application of eu law, which is necessary for the benefit of european union citizens, businesses and public administration. the commission looks forward to continuing its work with parliament on this very important topic.
member of the commission. – mr president, the commission thanks parliament for tabling this important initiative and appreciates the efforts undertaken to look into the implementation of the environmental liability directive (eld). the environmental liability directive sets the eu framework for prevention and remediation of damage to biodiversity, to water and to land. it is based on the ‘polluter pays’ principle. many findings and suggestions in the draft report and motion for a resolution are consistent with the commission’s own report and evaluation of the environmental liability directive in 2016. in the wake of that report, we are now working together with member states and stakeholders to improve their implementation of the directive, including developing the knowledge base, the expertise and administrative capacity for applying the directive. the shortcomings of the environmental liability directive need to be addressed: first, the unsatisfactory non—level playing field; second, the under-developed capacity; and third, the insufficient financial security level in many member states. the commission will work on these issues with the member states and stakeholders in the context of the multiannual work programme 2017-2020. the commission’s view is that the directive’s potential still needs to be fully exploited by making more use of it and by applying it more consistently. only after this should we consider legislative amendments. the systematic collection and analysis of data and information on environmental liability should lead to an eld information system shared with all interested member states. this would facilitate the future implementation of the directive. we will need to see how this initiative improves the implementation on the ground before considering extending the scope of the directive and before considering whether damage to air, or to health, should be covered. we have made a clear commitment to perform a new refit evaluation of the directive in four to six years’ time. that will provide the needed evidence base for future action. i thank parliament for supporting all efforts to improve the implementation of the environmental liability directive, especially in the framework of the multiannual work programme. thank you for your excellent report.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on the environment, public health and food safety. – mr president, sorry for being late; i was at the trilogue. the committee on the environment, public health and food safety (envi) participated, in accordance with rule 54, in the discussion on the environmental liability directive (eld). the eld is a key piece of legislation aiming to establish a framework of environmental liability based on the ‘polluter pays’ principle and to prevent and remedy environmental damage. as both the commission’s report and the committee on legal affairs highlighted, despite some improvements, the directive has shown incompleteness and thus limited effectiveness. implementation still varies significantly from one member state to another. we have a patchwork of liability systems, due also to the interplay with already—existing national laws, which in my opinion can undermine the common standards and expose some member states or regions to a greater risk of environmental disaster and the financial consequences thereof. as is clear from the envi opinion, better implementation of the directive, including improving the information base and providing more training and assistance to the member states – a key pillar of the commission’s action plan and multiannual work programme – is needed, but is far from being sufficient. we should address the remaining challenges in a systematic manner and revise the directive in the near future, in the light of the commission’s recent regulatory fitness and performance (refit) exercise. a revision would allow us to extend the scope and applicability of the directive by making operators of all activities strictly liable for all environmental damage they cause, which means removing the limitation in annex 3. in this context, the ini report rightly calls on the commission to impose liability for damage to human health and the environment in the future and also calls for the removal of certain exemptions. i also welcome very much the call for rethinking the financial security system, which is at the heart of the matter. this would entail high-level mandatory environmental liability insurance for operators, differentiated ceilings for activities with different risk factors and the creation of an eu—wide fund to bear the cost of relevant measures beyond the mandatory security in the event of large—scale disasters. i also stress that proper involvement of inspection bodies is key. in this context, the commission should further develop inspection support capacity at eu level and help the member states strengthen their national bodies. let me highlight one more message: we urgently need eu legislation on the minimum standards for implementing the aarhus convention ‘access to justice’ pillar.
mr president the principle behind the environmental liability directive is that the polluter pays. but in practice, there is another principle that many people think we see: that is, to privatize the huge profits and to shift the damage caused by pollution to society, to the population. we saw this for example in one of the biggest environmental disasters that happened in the recent history of europe, the red mud disaster in hungary. i congratulate my hungarian colleague, mr jávor benedek, who is well known for his commitment to environmental protection and is a good professional, on the report and the opinion. i would therefore like to ask him, given the particular case of the red mud disaster, to what extent he considered that the eu environmental liability directive was applicable and to what extent the lessons of the red mud disaster influenced this report? i strongly suspect that, for example, paragraphs 30 and 31 were influenced and motivated by all the deficiencies we have seen in the context of eu law in the area of liability related to the red crescent disaster.

in writing. my dear colleagues in the european parliament! demographic change is a decades-long societal problem with multiple and diverse causes, which requires an interdisciplinary approach and approach. it is clear that the fight against population decline must be included among the objectives of cohesion policy. in many cases, it is the economically disadvantaged regions that are most affected by population ageing, the emigration of young people from the labour force and negative natural reproduction. cohesion policy, while maintaining its essential role, can also make an effective contribution to the achievement of demographic objectives. at the same time, it is regrettable that the report mentions migration as a possible solution to reverse europe's population decline. i think it is a complete misconception that the backward regions of europe should be caught up in migration measures. in this respect, immigration policy cannot be placed before active family policy. the eu should rather look at how to re-emphasise the traditional family model and how to promote family-creating and family-supporting measures at eu level. i would mention hungary as a positive example, where these measures have been an integral part of government policy for some time.
in writing. the demographic composition of the european continent is changing, with an accelerating rate of population ageing. it is undeniable that this demographic change, the decline in the proportion of the working age population, places a significant economic, social and financial burden on member states, in particular with regard to the maintenance of the welfare state. the declining population requires a rethinking of member states' policies and the search for appropriate responses. many western european countries feel, as this report shows, that it is possible and desirable to compensate for the decline in their population by means of immigration. we in hungary disagree with that. we believe that immigration has many risks (e.g. security policy), integration is difficult and often unsuccessful, leading to the emergence of parallel societies. the solution to the demographic crisis is to increase family allowances, to put families and their children at the centre. the hungarian government is working to encourage more people to start a home and a large family. the figures speak for themselves: between 2010 and 2018, the amount of family allowances in the hungarian budget doubled, and the government declared 2018 the year of families.
in writing. the aim of the regulation amendment is to strengthen the consumer protection rights of eu citizens, with the aim of preventing cross-border infringements and enforcing the penalties imposed. it is the practice of fraudulent traders to move their headquarters to a member state where consumer protection is less developed, thereby avoiding legal consequences. in order to prevent such cases, it is necessary for member states to strengthen their cooperation in the field of consumer protection. member state authorities will be given additional powers such as the right to enter premises, search premises or buy samples, and new sanctions include the suspension or termination of a website/domain name. a common database will be set up to facilitate the exchange of information between member states in order to promote cross-border legal aid. the implementing authorities work in two-year cycles, which are also monitored by the commission every two years.

on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, according to key ngos, half of the eu’s natural areas are only protected on paper due to widespread implementation deficiencies. this action plan is an important step forward, as it aims to improve the practical implementation of the nature directives. but is it sufficient? is it comprehensive enough? despite the strong positive commitment to addressing the root problems, i think that the european commission has failed to propose actions to tackle the drivers of biodiversity loss, including agriculture, transport and energy. firstly, the commission must improve coherence with common agricultural policy and make sure that all infrastructure developments are fitted with proper environmental safeguards. financing is also a very important issue. according to an iep report, the current eu budget covers only up to 19% of the funding needed to properly manage natura 2000 sites. why does the action plan not better highlight the urgency of the need to scale up financial resources? biodiversity has to be mainstreamed in the eu budgets beyond 2020, with nature protection earmarked in each individual instrument in addition to the dedicated biodiversity scheme. my last remark: the eca found that the coordination between responsible authorities and other stakeholders in the member states was not sufficient; therefore, the commission should provide effective support to national and regional actors in the implementation of nature legislation and the improvement of environmental inspections.

author. – madam president, in february this year the commission published its first edition of the environmental implementation review (eir) to give an overview of the implementation of eu environmental law in each member state. the 28 country reports are accompanied by a summary document listing the common challenges, such as waste management, nature and biodiversity protection, air quality, noise pollution, water quality and management, to identify the root causes of problems, and providing policy proposals and recommendations for improvement. i would like to stress that the eir is a soft tool to shed light on implementation gaps, challenges and problems. in this sense it is a key to providing an early warning for the national and sub-national level policymakers which can take corrective actions and address the gaps in time. the eir also aims to demonstrate success stories that can serve as an example for other member states. the recently launched peer-to-peer tool facilitates this kind of exchange of knowledge or best practices. the exercise is to be repeated every two years, offering the possibility for iterative changes and improvements. we, members of the european parliament, welcome the eir and believe that this new review mechanism as a whole has the potential to improve compliance and policy implementation and thus also contribute to greater transparency and the credibility of the eu. yet, in order to fully realise this potential, we believe that the process could be further improved, including by broadening the scope of the review, as also expressed in the motion for a resolution adopted in october in the committee on the environment, public health and food safety. there is also a need for targeted assistance to the member states to overcome the barriers of proper, effective implementation, such as ineffective governance, inappropriate institutional framework, lack of capacity and an unclear responsibility of the authorities. my co-sponsors of the motion for a resolution and oral question – ms pietikäinen, ms bonafè, mr demesmaeker, mr gerbrandy, mr flanagan –and i tabled on behalf of the environment committee the following questions, first to the council, regarding the eir. parliament wishes to highlight the need to address the root causes, in particular lack of policy coherence and insufficient financing. the same goes for covering additional issues in the coming review cycles such as climate change, chemicals regulations, industrial emissions and other systemic challenges linked, for example, to energy, transport and regional policies. in order to demonstrate clear, strong political commitment, the council could hold regular meetings dedicated to the implementation of eu environmental law which could facilitate monitoring the performance and progress of a member state and ensure structured implementation dialogues as proposed by the commission. for the latter, member states should pay due attention to ensuring transparency and broad stakeholder participation. in addition, we would see added value in joint council meetings to address the implementation of cross-cutting issues and also the environmental challenges of a cross-border nature. we would also like to ask the commission this oral question and we raise some concerns about the exact means of following up on the policy proposals and recommendations for better implementation put forward by the commission. there are issues to be solved linked to data availability and comparability, especially in the context of the next review cycles that would hopefully run with a wider scope. we would like to hear the commission’s views on how the eir framework could be used as a means of assessing the implementation of the relevant sustainable development goals (sdgs) and the 2030 agenda and, in general, of shifting from the current sectoral approaches to a coherent, consistent and holistic solution. we put on the table tools, including a publicly available scoreboard to monitor the performance and progress of each member state and to improve transparency and raise public awareness and ensure proper public participation in each member state. last but not least, we would like to better understand the linkage between the eir process and the infringements and, in general, the exact actions taken by the commission in the event of non-compliance.

madam president, please. the commission has drawn up a draft regulation on the internal market in electricity in the context of the energy union. eight national parliaments objected to the regulation on the grounds that it violated the principle of subsidiarity. according to the hungarian parliament, decisions on capacity mechanisms will be taken at eu level, the definition of the energy mix will be indirectly taken away from the member states, and the member states' role as system operators will be narrowed. regional operational centres are established, with member states being responsible for security of supply. they unnecessarily restrict the power of member states to set prices, while giving them the means to protect consumer rights and reduce energy poverty. european energy cooperation should be structured in such a way as not to infringe the sovereignty of the member states as enshrined in the treaties.
in writing. the whole world is investing in renewable energy, the production of cars with combustion engines is being banned across europe, solar parks and wind farms are on the rise. what's all this for? because today renewable energy is the main driver of economic growth and because renewable energy is cheap. because it creates millions of jobs and because it ends our dependence on the russians. the newly adopted european renewable energy directive only further strengthens these. it obliges eu member states to source 35% of their total energy consumption from renewable sources. increasing renewables allows us to pay little or no for energy. for example, in germany, a leader in renewables, there have already been two cases where the consumer is paid for using electricity, rather than the other way around. the newly adopted directive also obliges member states to make it easier to install solar panels on our roofs. now in many countries, you have to wait years, you have to fill out hundreds of pages of forms to install a home solar panel. this will be done away with by the european parliament through a mandatory one-stop shop, on the basis of my own amendment. renewable energy is the future because it makes the cost of electricity cheaper, creates jobs, boosts the economy and, by the way, even saves the environment. europe is looking at these.

in writing. i would ask the honourable members to vote in favour of the text on amendments to the regulations in force in the field of agriculture and rural development. this adopted text is of paramount importance to our farmers. in all areas - producer organisations, greening, production-related and first pillar support for young farmers, the clearing of eu funds - significant relief has been achieved for european farmers and member state authorities. so, for example, i'd like to highlight two things here. on the one hand, we have strengthened the producer's bargaining position against the traders and processors who often abuse their dominance. similar to the provisions of the 2010 milk package, the recognised producer organisation will in future be able to plan production in other agricultural sectors in addition to the milk sector and negotiate supply contracts with traders on behalf of its members, thus becoming a stronger exception to the competition rules of union law. on the other hand, the greening rules, which were too bureaucratic and difficult to implement in practice, have been simplified in many respects. for example, in the future, ivory, cup plants and especially honey plants, which are very important to us for pollination and for keeping bees, will also be accounted for in the so-called ecologically important areas that have been taken out of management.

in writing. the panama papers revealed that the world's financial and political elite are trying to hide a significant portion of their wealth from the public through various offshore companies and countries. since then, countless similar cases have come to light, the paradise papers, the azeri money laundering, all based on the same logic: hiding assets of dubious origin, income, through a complex network of companies, so as not to have to account for and pay taxes. in the meantime, strict anti-money laundering rules are in place in the union and banks and banking supervisors have strict control systems. unfortunately, eu legislation does not cover all the gaps, but the transposition into national law, and even the enforcement and enforcement controls, are not adequate. basically because a web of corruption and financial interests is weaving through the world of economics and politics, exploiting the incoherence of the legislative background. to increase efficiency, the eu would have to take a number of decisions to tighten up the rules. in particular, trade links with legal entities established in tax havens should be prohibited where the ultimate beneficiary cannot be identified. publicity should be increased and guaranteed in relation to corporate networks. eu funds should not be paid to companies suspected of tax evasion and the participation of offshore companies in public procurement should be prohibited.

in writing. i believe that the european fund for strategic investments has achieved the objectives it set itself at its inception and that it is therefore justified to extend the programme. but if you look at the list of projects that have been completed and those that are still in progress, you might have a sense of emptiness. the esf has really only performed well in the western, more developed member states and regions, and we will find very little investment in the new member states and less developed regions, so more attention needs to be paid to geographical balance in the future. moreover, the mechanism offered by the esi funds should not serve as a basis for any reform of the structural funds after 2020. the preservation of the fundamental objectives of cohesion policy in accordance with article 179 of the treaty and the continued dominance of grants over financial instruments are the only way to ensure that economic disparities are effectively reduced.
in writing. the compromise proposal of the european parliament and the council increases the eu guarantee underpinning the european fund for strategic investments from eur 16 billion to eur 26 billion. the increase in the eu guarantee will increase the investment fund's investment portfolio to eur 500 billion by 2020. on the question of financing, we agree with the compromise text proposal, we support it, as it states that the european commission will respect the framework previously adopted in the mid-term review of the mff. the compromise strengthens the investment fund advisory platform, which will in future be able to employ experts at local level to help those interested in investment funds make their decisions. this could strengthen the diversification of the use of resources, i.e. that all european regions can benefit equally from the support provided by the investment fund. the investment fund will also be able to support motorway investments in the cohesion countries in the future, as agreed by parliament and the council. at the same time, we have managed to ensure that the operation of the investment fund will be subject to an independent review and the results achieved so far will be reviewed before any extension of the fund beyond 2020. in the interests of compromise, we can accept the amended draft of the european commission, since a council declaration was prepared for the agreement, which states that the agreement does not create a precedent for the future and respects the provisions of the treaty.
my fellow citizens! burma, known as myanmar since 1989, has undergone remarkable transformations since reforms began in 2011. the new government that came to power democratically in april 2016 after overthrowing decades of military dictatorship represents a historic opportunity for the country. the european union, with its own instruments, has played a decisive role in the process of change. in recent months, however, there has been legitimate concern about the violence perpetrated by the authorities against the large rohingya community in rakhine state, which has displaced hundreds of thousands of stateless persecuted people and forced them to flee to neighbouring bangladesh. the un high commissioner for human rights has rightly described the unsustainable situation as ethnic cleansing. in september this year, the european parliament adopted an emergency resolution condemning the myanmar government's appalling treatment of the rohingya minority. it called for the immediate investigation of serious human rights and ethnic violations and the prosecution of perpetrators as a precondition for the success of the democratic transition under way in the country and for further eu support.
madam president, please. i am pleased to note that the mcallister report is positive about the western balkans and gives due emphasis to addressing the security challenges facing the region. i also share the rapporteur's view that the stability and security of the western balkans must remain a priority for the eu as well. european integration processes have made a major contribution to maintaining regional peace and security. i particularly welcome the part of the report which suggests to the commission the need to increase the pre-accession funds, the ipa funds, since it is not viable for the european parliament to spend more annually on the maintenance of its two seats than, for example, the largest candidate country, serbia, received from the ipa to support all the reform measures it has undertaken.
madam president, please. it is also part of the political coherence of the european union that the aim of the union's efforts under discussion must be to guarantee european citizens the opportunity and security of living within a democratic legal framework based on our common values, and to strengthen the role of the european union as a global guiding force in this respect. but this can only be achieved by a genuine community effort if we are able to act together and effectively within the union against the violation of democratic rights, the desecration of european values and the phenomena of corrupt and authoritarian governance.
madam president, please. all those who spoke before me have already told us about the dramatic conditions of refugees in libya. they also told us that this life is simply unbearable, they also told us that we are not doing anything, that the situation is not getting better, but getting worse, and i think that's not really surprising, because libya is a country that really doesn't have a government with real political power. so refugees are completely exposed to the circumstances. local authorities are colluding with human traffickers, trampling on the rights of refugees, human rights. the question is what can the european parliament, the european union, do in this case? and i think that we have to come up with very clear and very specific demands. we have no time for any more delay, for talks, for negotiations, we have to demand from the libyan authorities, the libyan government, to end the arbitrary detention of refugees, to guarantee these people their human rights, to end the violence and the domination, to do everything to ensure that these people live in decent conditions. if we do not follow this path, nothing else will happen but that europe will lose its own conscience, betray its own values, degrade its own values, and nothing else will happen but that the forces of the extreme right will be strengthened.
i'll close this discussion. the commission shall be assisted by the committee established by article 15 of regulation (eu) no 182/2011.
the next item is the annual report 2016 by mrs godelieve quisthoudt-rowohl, on behalf of the committee on foreign affairs, on the situation of human rights and democracy in the world and the european union's policy on the matter (a8-0365/2017)
madam president, please. i would also like to congratulate you first of all on this comprehensive report, in which the author, on behalf of the european union, renews his commitment to the promotion of universal human rights, fundamental freedoms and values. the union must be able to promote respect for human rights beyond its borders in third countries. the report contains many examples of human rights violations. i think we can agree that the right to education and the right to speak one's mother tongue is a fundamental right. so i would like to draw attention to the fact that unfortunately in ukraine, in 2016, the process was launched that in the autumn of 2017 the new ukrainian education law, through the article on the language of education, would reduce the right of national minorities to use their mother tongue. while minorities have been able to access all levels of education in their mother tongue, after 2018 this will be limited to primary school, meaning the new law will take back rights already acquired. the eu's official position is that the existing rights of minorities cannot be reduced, as was stated in the eu-ukraine association council's final document last friday. the venice commission, in a report published on monday, said the criticism of article 7 was justified and proposed amendments. first, because the text adopted differs significantly from the one on which the minorities were consulted and agreed. article 7 of the law also contains significant ambiguities, although comprehensive reforms must take into account international and constitutional commitments, especially for existing minority rights. i hope that ukraine will fully implement the recommendations of the venice commission, consult with the minorities concerned and its neighbours, and incorporate the needs and proposals of the nationalities in the implementation of the education reform.
madam president, please. this afternoon, we have raised our voices in defense of the muslim rohingya in myanmar. in other cases, we defend the most persecuted christian communities in the world. the european union must act in a united way without ethnic or religious discrimination to respect individual human rights and the collective rights of minorities, as well as the freedoms of conscience, expression and religion. it is especially important that we take up the fight against disenfranchising and mass-murdering religious and political ideologies such as islamic fundamentalism and atheistic communism. the european union is the main sponsor of this universal struggle for human rights, as reflected in the parliament's 2016 human rights report.
i'll close this discussion. the vote will take place tomorrow, 13 december 2017, at 12 noon. the commission shall be assisted by the committee established by article 15 of regulation (eu) no 182/2011.
in writing. i congratulate the rapporteur on the fact that this year there was no need to submit an amendment in favour of people with disabilities, because the report specifically commends the ratification of the un convention on the rights of persons with disabilities and states that disability does not diminish human dignity and that it is therefore the duty of states to provide protection for people with disabilities. i agree that the principle of universal accessibility and the rights of persons with disabilities must be firmly embedded in all eu policies, underlining the prescriptive and horizontal nature of this issue. i support the eu's integration of the fight against discrimination on the basis of disability into its external action and development aid policies. i am pleased that the rights of persons with disabilities are included in the new european consensus on development policy. however, it is still relevant - as was stated in my motion last year - to examine more closely the effectiveness of projects relating to people with disabilities and to involve local ngos representing people with disabilities in their implementation. support for the social integration of people with disabilities in their own communities should be provided. local organisations should be involved, because they know exactly what is needed locally and they can help ensure that the support actually reaches the people for whom it is intended. we need to do more than talk, we need to do more than export democracy, we need to empower local communities.

madam president, when the history of brexit comes to be written, one aspect of it is bound to play a prominent role: the quality of the communication between london and brussels, especially in the media. ‘communication’ may be the wrong word; ‘miscommunication’ might be better. the council mandate of 29 april is crystal clear on what the eu expected from the united kingdom. yet, the british media have treated the negotiations as if they had never read the council’s mandate. who knows, maybe there is no ‘as if’ about it – certainly, much of the british media comment has reflected a very different mindset about brexit: one in which the eu’s position has played next to no role. it’s bizarre. the uk is constantly trumpeting diversity and multiculturalism. evidently, the eu, brussels, the european parliament and the 27 are not a part of this diversity.
in writing. jean claude juncker recently announced that the european union would provide substantial resources to help member states wishing to adopt the euro. of course, this requires a willingness to have a common currency. this eu initiative is a historic opportunity for hungary. europe would give money to modernize the hungarian economy and bring the standard of living back home closer to the west. we would have access to more eu funds and the hungarian economy would be protected by the full firepower of the eurozone. all this for free, not with our sweat. this requires only one thing: a commitment to the euro. of the 10 member states that joined in 2004, seven have already adopted the euro and the others are planning to do so, and recently the bulgarians and the czechs applied for observer status. however, the current hungarian government has no plans to introduce the single currency. this is a big mistake. the introduction of the euro would only bring advantages. the euro would reduce the public debt and eliminate exchange rate risk, which would help foreign exchange borrowers. the introduction of the euro would protect hungarian savings, not the weak hungarian bank, which is struggling to cope with the bankruptcy of a small rural savings cooperative. if there were a euro, hungarians could receive eu unemployment benefits within years. the introduction of the euro is in the interest of the hungarian people. now we're even getting paid for it. it's not worth missing.
in writing. i am following with grave concern the processes that have recently begun in the european council. while the commission and parliament are making significant efforts to create a common european asylum system that is responsive to current challenges, the european council seems to be characterised by day-to-day politics and cheap tactics. in the long run, the losers of this irresponsible delay will be the citizens of europe, and the winners will be the far-right parties. i am particularly concerned by the recent statement by president tusk, in which he calls into question the essential elements of the common european asylum policy. it is a grave mistake for the european council or its president to give in to the blackmail of a few governments led by far-right adventurers and turn their backs on the fundamental values of the european union. the concessions made to politicians who are unrestrainedly pushing for political gain do not create unity but, on the contrary, undermine the moral and legal foundations of the european union's unity. further delay, denial of the principle of solidarity, seeking unprincipled compromises, and questioning legitimate decisions afterwards will only lead to confusion and a weakening of trust in the european institutions. the citizens of europe expect the eu institutions to find solutions to the problems and to firmly reject the demagogy that undermines the european values.
in writing. the agenda for our current plenary debate reflects that the eu heads of state or government should again devote their week to reconciling the eu's interests and negotiating positions in the brexit negotiations. i think it is important to remind you at every such occasion that the irresponsible political tactics of a british right-wing government and the anti-european, populist, nationalist demagogy have created a situation which is unnecessarily hampering the construction of europe, to the detriment of all concerned. i think this is an important lesson to be borne in mind throughout the negotiation process, as the uk side tackles the problem it has created and the eu negotiators try to limit the damage to european citizens. in this context, i welcome the fact that at this stage of the negotiations, agreement in principle on the three essential framework conditions for moving forward has finally been reached. the european parliament will have a key role to play in ensuring that these agreements are fully and in good faith transposed into the future final agreement and that the civil and social rights of our citizens - whether they live and work in the member states or in the british isles - are fully respected during the agreed transition period and after brexit. as a hungarian representative, i am particularly concerned that many of my fellow citizens - who, as mostly young professionals, are seeking refuge in the uk from the economic restrictions and political constraints of the authoritarian orbán regime - and their families may not suffer further legal harm.
mr president i regret that i did not vote in favour of the report assessing the state of play of the brexit negotiations and setting out parliament's current position on the european union's positions. in assessing the brexit issue, i always take as a starting point the fact that this situation, which is unnecessarily hampering the european construction and damaging for all actors, was caused by the irresponsible political tactics of a british right-wing government. in this context, i welcome the fact that the report says that at the end of the current phase of the negotiations, there is an agreement in principle on the three essential framework conditions for moving forward. so, the mutual status of citizens, the financial settlement, the border with northern ireland, and that's pending approval by the next european council. but in the absence of concrete, sufficient concrete, i do not think that is enough.
mr president i welcome the fact that the european union has defined and declared its basic principles on social rights. i am convinced that effective and successful economic policy must always take precedence over social policy. the social protection of citizens will only be sustainable if it has an economic backing. if you have sustainable and non-credit-based economic growth and job creation, you can spend it on social causes and people will live better. the same is true of people with disabilities. i've been saying for years that we have to think of them as workers, too. hungary has been on the right track in this area since 2010 and the results speak for themselves. unemployment is around 4%, and the number of people in employment is the highest since the change of regime in 1989, with 4,445,000 people in work today, or 775,000 more than in 2010. so 68.7 percent of working-age adults are employed, 3.5 percent more than, say, in france. but the work is not over, we must continue to do everything we can for our citizens.
in writing. as a mother of four, i am delighted to see action taken at eu level against this lowest form of crime, which continues to victimize and endanger all our children and which, through the world of the internet, has penetrated into the most protected childrens' rooms. we can't waste any more time, because child pornography has a much wider range of victims than the public is aware of. an exemplary international effort is needed to tackle this phenomenon, as the internet knows no borders and it is not enough to tackle it at the level of individual member states. in particular, i would like to welcome the call in the report for member states to establish hotlines to help find missing children quickly and efficiently across borders. a very good example of this is amber alert europe, the european child rescue, alert and police network for missing children. according to the organisation, 250,000 children are reported missing in the union every year. in 76% of cases, the child's life is most at risk within three hours of the abduction. since law enforcement often stops at national borders, a rapid international response is essential, especially in border areas. effective cooperation and joint action between law enforcement, border protection and local authorities can help locate abducted children and save children's lives. child safety must remain a central issue in all eu member states. we must take care of our children.
in writing. i consider the implementation of the strategy for low-emission mobility to be an objective to be supported. i can agree with many of the proposals in the parliament's report, such as the increased use of natural gas, including lng and cng, in certain segments of transport. however, the parliament's report contains some proposals with which i strongly disagree. this includes the article which aims to introduce a minimum target for manufacturers for the share of zero-emission cars. i believe that mandatory regulation is an excessive interference in the economy, in the business decisions of economic operators. in fact, for certain operators, the requirement may result in a significant disadvantage in international competition. in the absence of such measures, incentive competition between producers is expected to develop, as is already the case. another point is the introduction of a journey-proportionate tariff for passenger cars. the commission's documentation also refers to this. while i fully support this principle in the case of lorries as the practical expression of the polluter pays principle , in the case of passenger cars i think there are other considerations which could override the said approach. i would also ask the commission to change its position.
mr president i fully agree with the commission's recommendations, all the more so because there are a number of member states in the european union which unfortunately cooperate with harmful practices. i strongly believe that it is not enough to investigate the tax environment alone, but i also believe it is important that member states prohibit offshore companies from entering the public sector in the area of public procurement.
mr president i voted in favour of the adoption of the report on the european union's common foreign and security policy last year and next year, appreciating its thorough and in many respects innovative and proactive nature. the report reflects well the way in which the european union will respond to the new security challenges in its environment, the changed content of the transatlantic relationship, the destabilisation of our mediterranean neighbourhood, international terrorism and the possible consequences of brexit, with concrete, innovative initiatives in the area of the common foreign and security policy in the period ahead. in the circumstances and on the basis of the arguments presented in the report, i accept, i consider it appropriate, that in the future additional funding will be needed to protect the eu and its member states by creating european strategic autonomy, eliminating overlaps, improving military mobility, implementing pesco, and increasing global responsibility through common foreign and security policy missions.
